On November 27, former president Martín Vizcarra considered that Sagasti's replacement in the senior ranks "is not legal" and that the Sagasti government must "respect the institutional framework."[^cite_note-205] On December 1, the former president Francisco Morales Bermúdez with former Defense Ministers Julio Velásquez Giacarini, Roberto Chiabra, Jorge Kisic Wagner, Jorge Moscoso, Walter Martos and Jorge Chávez Cresta, 12 former heads of the Joint Command of the Armed Forces and former general commanders of the Army, Navy and of the Air Force described that the change in the Police was "illegal" and maintained that the decision "is contrary to the legal system, affects the morale of the National Police of Peru and undermines the work that this institution carries out.[^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207]

On December 2, journalist Nicolás Lúcar released the testimony of a former member of the deactivated Special Intelligence Group (GEIN) who revealed that an alleged half-brother of the Minister of the Interior was a leader of the Shining Path terrorist group;[^cite_note-208][^cite_note-209] information denied by Minister Vargas.[^cite_note-210] A few hours later, Rubén Vargas Céspedes resigned from the Ministry of the Interior.[^cite_note-211] He was replaced by Cluber Aliaga Lodtmann.

On 12 March 2021, the prosecutor Germán Juárez Atoche requested preventive detention for 18 months for former president [Martín Vizcarra](51682201.md). This, within the framework of the investigation for the alleged crimes of aggravated collusion, improper passive bribery and illicit association to commit a crime.[^cite_note-212] The hearing was scheduled for 17 March,[^cite_note-213] where Judge María de los Ángeles Álvarez Camacho, after hearing both reasons from the prosecution and the defense of Vizcarra, was rejected [^cite_note-214] the request for preventive detention and appearance with restrictions was imposed.[^cite_note-215]

#### 2021 elections and controversy

Main article: [2021 Peruvian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Peruvian_general_election)



[Pedro Castillo](49637220.md), the unexpected winner of the 2021 elections. It wasn't until the very end that he appeared in the polls.

The 2021 elections contained the most candidates at that time, containing almost 18 candidates. Among them were [Pedro Castillo](49637220.md), who had led the 2017 Teacher's strike; [Keiko Fujimori](4643445.md); and businessman [Rafael López Aliaga](65483323.md). Castillo's campaign with the Communist [Free Peru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Peru) Party, which often promoted the views of [Fidel Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro) and [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez), brought controversy due to their views [regarding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_incidents_of_xenophobia_during_the_Venezuelan_refugee_crisis) [Venezuelans](19835614.md) in the country,[^cite_note-216][^cite_note-217][^cite_note-218] as well as campaigning for the expulsion of [DEA](146720.md) agents[^cite_note-219] and the pardon of rebel leader [Antauro Humala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antauro_Humala).[^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221]

On 23 May, a mass killing of eighteen people occurred in [San Miguel del Ene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Miguel_del_Ene_attack), a rural area in the [Vizcatán del Ene District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vizcat%C3%A1n_del_Ene_District) of [Satipo Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satipo_Province).[^cite_note-222] Along with the corpses, some of which were burned, [leaflets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pamphlet) signed by the [MPCP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Militarized_Communist_Party_of_Peru) were found, featuring the [hammer and sickle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hammer_and_sickle) and defining the attack as a [social cleansing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_cleansing) operation. The leaflets also called for a boycott of the 6 June elections and accused those who voted for [Keiko Fujimori](4643445.md) and her [Popular Force](15247524.md) party of [treason](31292.md). The military quickly accused Shining Path of the attack, although they were allegedly referring to the MCPC. However, no formal investigation had been performed before the links to Shining Path were claimed. [OjoPúblico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OjoP%C3%BAblico) described the media release by the military as "an inaccurate reference to the Shining Path."

The attack and subsequent media coverage would provide increased support for Fujimori, whose rhetoric aligned Castillo with armed communists.[^cite_note-The_Economist-2021-223] The Fujimori campaign used the attack as a springboard for support, pointing to alleged ties between MOVADEF, a Shining Path political group, and Castillo, attempting to align him to the attack. Fujimori expressed condemnation against the attack during a press conference in [Tarapoto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarapoto) as well as regret that "bloody acts" still happened in the country and her condolences to the relatives of the victims.[^cite_note-224] Pedro Castillo also condemned the killings during a rally in [Huánuco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hu%C3%A1nuco), expressing solidarity towards the relatives of the victims and also urging the National Police to investigate the attack to clarify the events.[^cite_note-225] [Vladimir Cerrón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Cerr%C3%B3n), Secretary General of Free Peru, stated that "the right-wing needed \[Shining] Path to win"; Cerrón deleted the tweet moments later while condemning any act of terrorism.[^cite_note-226] Prime Minister [Nuria Esparch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuria_Esparch), who held the position of the [Ministry of Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defense_\(Peru\)), condemned the attack and guaranteed that the electoral process would take place normally.[^cite_note-227]

The election also saw the emergence of many [far-right](221220.md) candidates.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsensioCamachoGonzálezGrompone202127–71-24] Regarding the first round of presidential elections, Javier Puente, assistant professor of Latin American Studies at [Smith College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smith_College) in the [North American Congress on Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Congress_on_Latin_America) wrote: "With a baffling number of candidates – 18 in total – the 2021 presidential ballot included convicted felons, presumed money launderers, xenophobes, a fascist billionaire, an overrated and outdated economist, a retired mediocre footballer, a person accused of murdering a journalist, and other colorful figures. The vast majority of candidates represented the continuation of the neoliberal economic model that has been responsible for decades of meager financial performance and unequal growth."[^cite_note-Puente-2021-228] Puente stated that only three leftist candidates proposed alternatives to the neoliberal politicians (Veronika Mendoza, Marco Arana, and Pedro Castillo), describing Castillo as "far from being a 'comrade' who will champion leftist demands, Castillo is the new face of an anti-system impulse. ... Only in a neoliberal system that outcasts any form of market dissent as radical would a figure like Castillo acquire a role as a leftist."[^cite_note-Puente-2021-228]

The *[Americas Quarterly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americas_Quarterly)* argues that such behavior resulted with less support for the leftist candidate Verónika Mendoza and promoted [political polarization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_polarization) within Peru.[^cite_note-Deutsche_Welle-2020-149] With the ongoing political crisis that saw in the span of two years the [dissolution of the Congress of Peru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%E2%80%932020_Peruvian_constitutional_crisis) and the removal of three presidents ([Pedro Pablo Kuczynski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Pablo_Kuczynski), [Martín Vizcarra](51682201.md), and [Manuel Merino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Merino)), concerns were raised among analysts about the increased political polarization's relationship with Peru's democratic stability.[^cite_note-Andina-150] Lead researcher of pollster [Institute of Peruvian Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_Peruvian_Studies), Patricia Zárate, stated: "I think the scenario that's coming is really frightening."[^cite_note-Andina-150]

As the second round of elections approached, Fujimori's campaign used [fearmongering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fearmongering) tactics to gain support of the middle and upper classes in Lima, accusing Castillo of attempting to institute communism in Peru and to follow the path of Hugo Chávez and Nicolas Maduro in Venezuela.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsensioCamachoGonzálezGrompone202127–71-24] Some scholars have recognized the similarities of Fujimori and Castillo; both are [cultural conservatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_conservatives) opposing [same-sex marriage](92656.md) and abortion, as campaigning for the second round of elections began.[^cite_note-Public_Radio_International-229] Olga González, associate dean of the Kofi Annan Institute for Global Citizenship at [Macalester College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macalester_College), stated that the situation is more complex than "binaries" between social classes, although she acknowledged that such dichotomies "speak to how polarized the country is."[^cite_note-Public_Radio_International-229]

In the second round of elections, Peru's major media networks were accused of aligning with Fujimori to discredit Castillo. Some news media allegedly disseminated [fake news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news) against Castillo.[^cite_note-RPP-2019-137][^cite_note-The_Guardian-2021-25] *[Le Monde diplomatique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Monde_diplomatique)* wrote, "The privately owned media torpedoed Castillo incessantly with fake news, and not without rattling the Shining Path [scarecrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Straw_man)". International media also stated that Peruvian news organizations polished Fujimori's image and praised her, as well as assisting her media campaign tactic which included attacks accusing Castillo of being linked to armed [communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist) groups.[^cite_note-The_Economist-2021-223] *The Guardian* described accusations linking Castillo to Shining Path as "incorrect", while the [Associated Press](18935732.md) said that allegations by Peruvian media of links to Shining Path were "unsupported."[^cite_note-230][^cite_note-231]

With the support of [Mario Vargas Llosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mario_Vargas_Llosa) and his son [Álvaro Vargas Llosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%81lvaro_Vargas_Llosa)'s neoliberal Fundación International para la Libertad (FIL), Fujimori attempted to reshape her image as being more democratic.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsensioCamachoGonzálezGrompone202113–24-232] Vargas Llosa ran and lost against Alberto Fujimori in Peru's [1990 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_Peruvian_general_election),[^cite_note-233] and had previously criticized Fujimori, making statements such as "the worst option is that of Keiko Fujimori because it means the legitimation of one of the worst dictatorships that Peru has had in its history"[^cite_note-234] and that "Keiko is the daughter of a murderer and a thief who is imprisoned, tried by civil courts with international observers, sentenced to 25 years in prison for murder and theft. I do not want her to win the elections."[^cite_note-235][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsensioCamachoGonzálezGrompone202113–24-232]



An [anti-fujimorist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Fujimorism) manifestation in Lima on 22 May following [Keiko Fujimori](4643445.md)'s attempt to obstruct the election

After Castillo took the lead during the ballot-counting process, Fujimori promoted unproven claims of electoral fraud.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsensioCamachoGonzálezGrompone202191-236][^cite_note-Collyns-2021-237][^cite_note-Swissinfo-2021-238][^cite_note-France_24-2021-239] In a media event following election day, Fujimori alleged that a "series of irregularities" had occurred, presenting photographs and videos in an attempt to support her allegations, while also accusing Free Peru of attempting to "distort and delay" the election process.[^cite_note-Collyns-2021-237][^cite_note-France_24-2021-239] Fujimori argued that it consisted in the challenge of polling stations where Fujimori would register a greater number of votes than his opponent, previous training talks by Free Peru in which they ask their representatives to arrive early at the polling stations to ensure control of the polling stations where titular members did not attend and irregularities in the vote count.[^cite_note-240] To support the complaints, Keiko's running mate, [Luis Galarreta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luis_Galarreta), assured that Free Peru did a "high number of challenges" to electoral acts in which Keiko was favoured so they could not be counted to the final estimate until they were evaluated first by the National Jury of Elections.[^cite_note-241] According to the complaint, over 1 300 voting acts were challenged by Free Peru;[^cite_note-Swissinfo-2021-238] however, the first claims were rebutted by national electoral entities.[^cite_note-Swissinfo-2021-238] After the resolution of the challenged votes and the acts observed by the Special Electoral Juries, the National Office of Electoral Processes published the total results on 15 June, in which Pedro Castillo surpassed Keiko Fujimori in number of votes.[^cite_note-242]

According to *The Guardian*, various international observers countered Fujimori's claims, stating that the election process was conducted in accordance with international standards.[^cite_note-Collyns-2021-237] Observers from the Inter-American Union of Electoral Organizations, the [Organization of American States](22788.md), and the [Progressive International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressive_International) denied any instances of widespread fraud and praised the accuracy of the elections.[^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244] *The Guardian* also reported that analysts and political observers criticized Fujimori's remarks, noting that it made her appear desperate after losing her third presidential run in a ten-year period.[^cite_note-Collyns-2021-237] Fernando Tuesta, political scientist from the Pontifical Catholic University of Peru, stated: "It's extremely regrettable that when the result is not favourable, that the candidate talks about fraud. It's terrible, ... They have been talking about fraud because they don't want to respect the result."[^cite_note-Collyns-2021-237] On 9 June, Fujimori sought to have around 200,000 votes annulled and for 300,000 votes to be reviewed.[^cite_note-245]

Following reports of Castillo's apparent victory, Fujimori and her supporters made unsubstantiated claims of [electoral fraud](382165.md), leading [obstructionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obstructionist) efforts to overturn the election with support of wealthy citizens of Lima.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsensioCamachoGonzálezGrompone202169–71-246][^cite_note-Swissinfo-2021-238][^cite_note-The_Guardian-2021-25][^cite_note-Collyns-2021-237][^cite_note-The_Washington_Post-247][^cite_note-Applebaum-2021-248] The economic and political elites refused to recognize Castillo's ascent to the presidency,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsensioCamachoGonzálezGrompone202127–71-24] with those among the more affluent, including former military officers and wealthy families, demanded new elections, promoted calls for a [military coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_coup), and utilized [classist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classist) or [racist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racist) rhetoric to support their allegations of fraud.[^cite_note-Swissinfo-2021-238] According to analysts, Peru was more susceptible to unrest as a result of Fujimori's narrative since democratic institutions are weaker in the nation.[^cite_note-Swissinfo-2021-238] Minor clashes occurred in Lima between Fujimori supporters from the capital city and Castillo supporters from other regions, with [rondas campesinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rondas_campesinas) equipped with sticks and machetes arriving in the [Historic Centre of Lima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historic_Centre_of_Lima) to defend Castillo's election and dissuade Fujimori protesters.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsensioCamachoGonzálezGrompone202169–71-246] Fujimori's response to Castillo's victory was also to portray her movement as defending democracy and assumed a nationalist image, adopting the [Peru national football team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peru_national_football_team) uniform and colors during rallies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsensioCamachoGonzálezGrompone202113–24-232] This intensified political polarization between urban and rural Peruvians, portraying rich, white individuals as democratic while identifying indigenous poor individuals as communists.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsensioCamachoGonzálezGrompone202113–24-232]



Results of the first round of the election

After exit polls gave the victory to Keiko Fujimori over Pedro Castillo, supporters of Free Peru mobilized to the offices of the ONPE to protest against a possible fraud against their candidate,[^cite_note-249] bringing banners saying "no to the fraud."[^cite_note-250] From [Tacabamba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacabamba_District), [Cajamarca](554990.md),[^cite_note-251] Castillo called upon his followers and supporters on Twitter to "defend the votes" and go to the streets to "defend democracy."[^cite_note-252] Protests against Fujimori and an alleged fraud took place in cities such as [Juliaca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juliaca), [Puno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puno), and [Ilave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilave).[^cite_note-253]

After the publication of the quick count and the first official results, protests by supporters of both Free Peru and Popular Force took place.[^cite_note-254] Amid the fraud accusations and the final vote count, there were nearly daily protests and marches, mostly in the capital [Lima](85423.md).[^cite_note-255] Besides Fujimori supporters, groups opposed to Castillo, mobilized by the fear of communism or aversion to the left wing, mobilized asking for the annulment of the elections.[^cite_note-El_Espectador-2021-256] Among the opposition groups there were anti-communists, far-right followers, and [fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascist) groups, including members of [Legionary Action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legionary_Action?action=edit\&redlink=1) ('Acción Legionaria').[^cite_note-El_Espectador-2021-256] During a mobilization in the San Martín Square in Lima, [*rondas campesinas*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronda_Campesina) supportive of Castillo carried [machetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machete) with them.[^cite_note-257] On 14 July, several pro-Fujimori protesters gathered at the Government Palace demanding an audit of the election. Protesters clashed with the [National Police of Peru](14896441.md), and [Health Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Health_\(Peru\)) [Óscar Ugarte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%93scar_Ugarte) and [Housing Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Housing,_Construction_and_Sanitation_\(Peru\)) Solangel Fernández were attacked during the protests.[^cite_note-rpp.pe-2021-258][^cite_note-elmundo.es-259] On 15 July, Sagasti reaffirmed that there was no evidence of voter fraud.[^cite_note-andina.pe-2021-260]

Senior fellow of the [Washington Office on Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Office_on_Latin_America) Jo Marie Burt described the overturn attempts as "a slow-motion conspiracy to prevent Castillo from becoming president", with *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* reporting that if Castillo was prevented from becoming president by 28 July 2021, a new election would be initiated.[^cite_note-RPP-2019-137] Fujimori's statements about possibly overturning the election, along with her use of [fake news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news) and legal challenges, were also described as being inspired by the [attempts to overturn the 2020 U.S. presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attempts_to_overturn_the_2020_U.S._presidential_election) by former U.S. president [Donald Trump](4848272.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsensioCamachoGonzálezGrompone202191-236][^cite_note-Swissinfo-2021-238][^cite_note-The_Guardian-2021-25][^cite_note-The_Washington_Post-247][^cite_note-Applebaum-2021-248] According to [Cornell University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornell_University) professor of Latin American politics Kenneth Roberts, "\[w]hen the credibility is called into question the way it has been by Trump and the Republicans in the U.S., it creates a bad example that other leaders and countries can follow, providing a template to change results they don't like."[^cite_note-The_Washington_Post-247][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAsensioCamachoGonzálezGrompone202113–24-232]

To avoid the questions of election legitimacy, election authorities in Peru approved the use of [election monitoring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_monitoring).[^cite_note-Ojo_Público-2021-261] In total, one hundred and fifty observers (ninety-nine in Peru and fifty-one abroad) were approved to observe elections throughout Peru.[^cite_note-Ojo_Público-2021-261] The origin of the observers were from twenty-two different countries, with thirty-five observers from the [Organization of American States](22788.md), while others were from Argentina, Colombia, Costa Rica, Ecuador, El Salvador, Guatemala, Mexico, Panama, Paraguay, Spain, Switzerland, the United States, and Uruguay.[^cite_note-Ojo_Público-2021-261] Observer approval required providing election authorities observation plans; these plans included protocols to inform authorities of crimes, violations of electoral law or any complaints they collected.[^cite_note-Ojo_Público-2021-261] Observers were then responsible with providing an official, final report to authorities.[^cite_note-Ojo_Público-2021-261] According to [OjoPúblico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OjoP%C3%BAblico), "the observers carry out the review of the activities of election day, ranging from the installation of the voting tables, the conditioning of the secret chambers, the conformity of the ballots, the minutes, the amphorae and any other electoral material, to the counting, the counting of the vote and the transfer of the electoral records at the end of the day."[^cite_note-Ojo_Público-2021-261]

On 10 June, the Peruvian Prosecution asked for the detention of Keiko on charges of violating the conditional liberty that she was granted during the open criminal process against her.[^cite_note-262] On 17 June, Fujimori repeated claims of voter fraud.[^cite_note-263] On 28 June, Fujimori traveled to the [Government Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_Palace_\(Peru\)) and personally delivered demands to President [Francisco Sagasti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Sagasti) to initiate an audit of the results by international entities.[^cite_note-Reuters-2020-12] On 30 June, several members of the [Popular Force](15247524.md) party traveled to the [OAS Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan_American_Union_Building) in [Washington, D.C.](108956.md), to publicize the voter fraud claims, with sociologist Francesca Emanuele condemning them as "coup plotters" during a press conference.[^cite_note-264][^cite_note-265] On 2 July, Sagasti rejected a request to audit the second round of the election, and Fujimori accused Sagasti of abdicating his "great responsibility to ensure fair elections."[^cite_note-266] On 19 July, Fujimori admitted her defeat but reaffirmed that "votes were stolen" from her.[^cite_note-U.S._News_&_World_Report-267] On 18 June, former Supreme Court President Javier Villa Stein filed a complaint for protection by describing the ballot vote as "questioned", arguing an alleged "electoral process flawed by various acts that undermine the popular will" and asking the judiciary branch to "declare the election void."[^cite_note-268] Lawyer Renán Galindo Peralta requested that it be rejected outright considering it inadmissible because it did not fall under the Organic Law of Elections and because the judicial branch lacked the powers to annul elections.[^cite_note-269]

On 23 June, Luis Arce, a judge on the [National Jury of Elections](5445567.md) (JNE), resigned and alleged bias on the jury which had rejected ten Fujimori requests to annul Castillo votes. On its Twitter account, JNE rejected Arce's allegation of bias as "offensive", and said its judges were not allowed to resign in the middle of reviews of cases, so he would be suspended instead, and a provisional replacement found "to avoid delaying our work." Castillo's Free Peru party said the resignation was aimed at "preventing the proclamation of Pedro Castillo, thereby ignoring the popular vote, breaking democracy and installing a coup d'état with silk gloves." In the wake of Arce's resignation, a lawyer representing Fujimori said that the government should consider asking the [Organization of American States](22788.md) (OAS) to audit the electoral process, as was done during the [2019 Bolivian political crisis](62307909.md). The OAS stated that its mission to the country had not found any issues in the conduct of the election.[^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271] Former candidate [George Forsyth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Forsyth_\(footballer\)) attributed it as part of the preparation of a "coup d'etat" and that Arce himself was "attacking democracy."[^cite_note-272]

On 25 June, former 2001 and 2016 presidential candidate [Fernando Olivera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Olivera_\(politician\)) revealed audio tapes, alleging that [Vladimiro Montesinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimiro_Montesinos), who was the right-hand man to former President [Alberto Fujimori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Fujimori) currently serving a prison sentence for crimes committed during the Fujimori period, was behind the attempts for Keiko Fujimori to be declared as the winner of the second round against Castillo, including through the payment of bribes to electoral officials and influencing the [National Jury of Elections](5445567.md). The [National Penitentiary Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Penitentiary_Institute_\(Peru\)) and [Peruvian Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peruvian_Navy) announced an investigation, confirming that Montesinos made two phone calls from Callao Naval Base where he is jailed, on 10 and 23 June to unauthorised persons coordinating the effort to overturn the election.[^cite_note-gestion.pe-273]

Further reports showed that Montesinos allegedly was able to make seventeen phone calls from a [landline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landline) phone to retired military officer Pedro Rejas, reportedly suggesting to Rejas that bribes needed to be paid and that Fujimori's husband, an American, go to the United States embassy in Lima to present "documentation of the fraud" to the Office of Regional Affairs and [Central Intelligence Agency](5183633.md).[^cite_note-RPP-2019-137][^cite_note-IDL_Reporteros-2021-274] According to [IDL-Reporteros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IDL-Reporteros), Montesinos suggests that the documentation would reach President [Joe Biden](145422.md) and that his administration would condemn the election as interference from Cuba, Nicaragua, and Venezuela, subsequently giving Fujimori's claims of fraud more weight.[^cite_note-IDL_Reporteros-2021-274] Rumors also spread of a possible [coup d'état](25914154.md) against Castillo, if he were declared the official winner of the presidential election.[^cite_note-The_Guardian-2021-25] A letter signed by almost one-hundred retired officers, including [Francisco Morales Bermúdez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Morales_Berm%C3%BAdez), of the Peruvian armed forces was written calling on current military leaders in Peru to refuse recognizing the election of Castillo into the presidency.[^cite_note-The_Guardian-2021-25] President Sagasti condemned the letter, stating: "They want to incite top commanders of the Army, Navy, and Air force to break the rule of law."[^cite_note-The_Guardian-2021-25]

On 14 July, several pro-Fujimori protesters gathered at the Government Palace demanding an audit of the election. Protesters, many of which were members of the [neofascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-fascism) [La Resistencia Dios, Patria y Familia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Resistencia_Dios,_Patria_y_Familia), clashed with the [National Police of Peru](14896441.md), and [Health Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Health_\(Peru\)) [Óscar Ugarte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%93scar_Ugarte) and [Housing Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Housing,_Construction_and_Sanitation_\(Peru\)) Solangel Fernández were attacked during the protests.[^cite_note-rpp.pe-2021-258][^cite_note-elmundo.es-259] On 15 July, Sagasti reaffirmed that there was no evidence of voter fraud.[^cite_note-andina.pe-2021-260]

On 19 July, Fujimori admitted her defeat but reaffirmed that "votes were stolen" from her.[^cite_note-U.S._News_&_World_Report-267]

### Castillo presidency



Castillo and his first cabinet, led by [Guido Bellido](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guido_Bellido).

On 20 August 2021, Congress voted against a proposal to declare the former Bolivian president [Evo Morales](284163.md) a [persona non grata](521241.md).[^cite_note-275] However, on 22 November 2021, the Foreign Relations Commission of Congress, led by [Popular Force](15247524.md), agreed to declare Evo Morales a persona non grata for "his negative political activism" and "political interference" in Peruvian territory, referring to repeated trips Morales undertook to rally support for Castillo's agenda. The Foreign Relations Commission (FRC) also questioned Morales' protection by state security and use of official vehicles. The FRC's resolution urged authorities to take "necessary actions" to prohibit Morales from entering Peru.[^cite_note-276]

Almost immediately after his appointment as Foreign Minister, the media began reporting on [Héctor Béjar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%A9ctor_B%C3%A9jar)'s past. Béjar had been part of the [National Liberation Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Liberation_Army_\(Peru\)) during the 1960s and had met [Che Guevara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Che_Guevara) according to his book "Peru 1965: Notes on a Guerrilla Experience." In a video published in November 2020, Béjar had said that 'terrorism began in the Navy' and that 'the [Shining Path](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shining_Path) was trained by the CIA', leading to significant public backlash.[^cite_note-277] Later, the Peruvian Navy put out a statement about wanting Béjar to retract, and claimed that Béjar's statement were an affront against those who fought against terrorism.[^cite_note-278] Following that, Congress started an [interpellation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interpellation_\(politics\)) process against Béjar, which was seen by manyas a [show trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Show_trial) that would be almost certainly followed by impeachment. However, Béjar resigned before the interpellations began. According to Béjar, Prime Minister [Guido Bellido](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guido_Bellido) asked him to resign to prevent him from speaking before Congress, which he said was a way of censoring him.[^cite_note-279] Béjar held the position of Foreign Minister for just 19 days. His replacement was the career diplomat [Óscar Maúrtua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%93scar_Ma%C3%BArtua).

After his resignation, Béjar posted "I am free again!!!" on [Facebook](7529378.md).[^cite_note-280] Later, he would claim that this was the beginning of a [soft coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soft_coup) against the Castillo's government.[^cite_note-Público-281] Béjar also claimed that there is a group in the Navy and the extreme right that does not want an independent foreign policy.[^cite_note-282] He also claimed that his phrases were distorted and taken out of context. Béjar stated that his expressions on the Navy were about a bomb plot against the Minister of War in 1975, and an attack against two Cuban fishing boats off the coast of Peru in 1977. Declassified documents show that the US Embassy suspected that these attacks were carried out by right-wing sectors of the Navy, which were unhappy with the military junta's close ties to Cuba at the time.[^cite_note-Público-281][^cite_note-283]

To resolve the renewed waves of protests, a delegation led by then Prime Minister [Guido Bellido](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guido_Bellido) arrived to [Tambobamba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tambobamba) on 17 September. The Prime Minister pledged to negotiate a solution between the mining communities and MMG Limited within two months.[^cite_note-284] However, two weeks later the miners grew upset over a perceived lack of progress, and thus resumed their strike.[^cite_note-285]

The Free Peru parliamentarian Margot Palacios spoke out against the FRC's resolution, saying that "Only a colonial mentality, narrow, premodern, medieval and discriminatory, can deny entry to Peru to a brother who has done much for the Andean culture, of which Peruvians and Bolivians are part of. For several centuries, we were one state: the Tahuantinsuyo."[^cite_note-286]

On 3 October, the 53rd anniversary of [Juan Velasco's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Velasco_Alvarado) rise to power via a [military coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_Peruvian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), Castillo announced the start of the Second Agrarian Reform with the aim of helping small farmers and ending abuse and inequality. This was a tacit acknowledgement of the original Agrarian Reform carried out by Velasco. Castillo stated that the Second Agrarian Reform will respect property rights and will not involve the expropriation of land. However, Castillo also stated "Let's end with the bosses and the landowners. They will no longer eat thanks to the sweat of the poor and of the peasants."[^cite_note-287]

On 22 October 2021, the government presented a lawsuit before the Constitutional Court (CT) that Congress' reinterpretation of the question of confidence was unconstitutional.[^cite_note-288] The CT unanimously admitted the case on 28 October 2021.[^cite_note-289] On 2 November 2021, Eloy Espinosa-Saldaña, who was appointed [rapporteur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapporteur) of the case, asked Congress to quickly respond to the lawsuit. Meanwhile, the Minister of Justice, Aníbal Torres, claimed that if the CT respected its past judgements, it should declare the unconstitutionality of the law that reinterprets the question of trust.[^cite_note-290]

On 4 November, the General Commander of the Army, José Vizcarra Álvarez, and Jorge Luis Chaparro, the General Commander of the Air Force, were relieved from their duties. Both had served in their positions for three months prior to their removal.[^cite_note-291] On 8 November 2021, Vizcarra claimed that President Castillo, Defense Minister [Walter Ayala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Ayala), and presidential secretary Bruno Pacheco had pressured him to irregularly promote two [colonels](199023.md). Vizcarra claimed that his refusal to promote them led to him being removed as General Commander of the Army.[^cite_note-292]

On 6 November, Free Peru's Regional Secretary Jorge Spelucín claimed that President Castillo had cut off all communication channels with Free Peru. Spelucín claimed that the President's environment was guiding him towards the political center and right, and that Free Peru wanted to help Castillo return to the left. Former Prime Minister Guido Bellido also affirmed that Castillo had cut off communication after the resignation of the Bellido cabinet.[^cite_note-293] Bellido also claimed that Castillo's ideological formation "is not from the left," but rather at "the level of a basic trade unionist."[^cite_note-294]

Subsequently, members from the Congressional opposition denounced the alleged irregularities. They began to prepare a motion of [interpellation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interpellation_\(politics\)) against the Defense Minister, which would allow Congress to question him on the incident. Jorge Montoya, the spokesperson of [Popular Renewal](66197743.md), stated that following the interpellations he would impeach Ayala. Afterward, Ayala stated that he was evaluating resigning as Minister of Defense due to "continuous attacks." However, he denied claims that he had interfered with the promotions.[^cite_note-295]

Afterward, several other generals affirmed that the Secretary of the Presidency had interfered with promotions.[^cite_note-296] Chaparro, the former commander of the Air force, claimed that he had been asked on 3 occasions to promote people who did not have the necessary skills.[^cite_note-297] The leader of Free Peru, [Vladimir Cerrón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Cerr%C3%B3n), distanced his party from the scandal, saying that Free Peru does not lead the current government.[^cite_note-298] Meanwhile, Prime Minister [Mirtha Vásquez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirtha_V%C3%A1squez) gave a statement, saying that the government respects an attitude of non-interference in the Armed Forces. She stated that "serious" facts were revealed, and that a decision would be announced within the next few hours.[^cite_note-299]

On 8 November, the Interior Ministry claimed that groups consisting of ex-military and ex-police personnel are appearing, many of whom being veterans of the [war against the Shining Path](9624647.md). Representatives from said groups and other related groups claimed to have pacifist intentions, citing plans for a march and their right to "freely defend themselves and protest."[^cite_note-larepublica.pe-2021-300]

On 13 November, Justice Minister [Aníbal Torres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An%C3%ADbal_Torres) called on Walter Ayala to resign.[^cite_note-301] Meanwhile, Health Minister [Hernando Cevallos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hernando_Cevallos) stated that Castillo was very annoyed with presidential secretary Bruno Pacheco's behavior, and that he had removed those people who may violate the independence of the armed forces.[^cite_note-302] Additionally, rumors grew that Prime Minister [Mirtha Vásquez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mirtha_V%C3%A1squez) might resign due to the controversy. In Peruvian law, the resignation of the PM dissolves the entire cabinet. Responding to the rumors, Labor Minister Betssy Chávez said that there was "a lot of speculation" on the matter, but affirmed that the cabinet would work together.[^cite_note-303] Meanwhile, Aníbal Torres downplayed such rumors, saying that he hadn't seen "differences" between Castillo and Vásquez.[^cite_note-304]

On 14 November, Walter Ayala announced his irrevocable resignation.[^cite_note-305] However, the next day, Ayala stated that the resignation would only become effective once Castillo accepted it, and that he would continue working in the meanwhile. Ayala stated that he could not abandon his post since there was no replacement as of then, and accused Congress of not allowing him to work.[^cite_note-306] Later that day, Castillo accepted the resignation of Walter Ayala and thanked him for his services.[^cite_note-307] On 17 November, Juan Carrasco was sworn in as the new Defense Minister. Carrasco had previously been Castillo's Interior Minister during the Bellido cabinet.[^cite_note-308]

In December 2021, prosecutor José Domingo Pérez reported that Keiko Fujimori received one million dollars from the Odebrecht Department of Structured Operations that was delivered through offshore intermediary accounts of Gonzalo Monteverde's company Construmaq.[^cite_note-El_Búho-2021-309][^cite_note-310] Domingo stated that he held 1,900 pieces of evidence to determine that a criminal group existed within Popular Force.[^cite_note-El_Búho-2021-309]

#### First removal attempt

Presented in visitor documents as a lobbyist for the construction company Termirex, [Karelim López](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karelim_L%C3%B3pez) met with Castillo's chief of staff Bruno Pacheco multiple times.[^cite_note-IDL_Reporteros-2021-2-311] In November 2021, four months into his term, Keiko Fujimori announced that her party was pushing forward [impeachment](15328.md) proceedings, arguing that Castillo was "morally unfit for office".[^cite_note-312] That day, investigators raided the Government Palace during an [influence peddling](630570.md) investigation and found that Pacheco had US\$20,000 present in his office's bathroom.[^cite_note-BBC_News-2021-313][^cite_note-Al_Jazeera-2021-314] Pacheco said that the money was part of his savings and salary, though he resigned from his position in order to prevent the scandal from affecting Castillo.[^cite_note-BBC_News-2021-313] On 25 November 28 legislators from Fujimori's party presented a signed motion of impeachment to congress, setting up a vote for opening impeachment proceedings against Castillo.[^cite_note-Al_Jazeera-2021-314] A short time later, controversy arose when newspapers reported that Castillo had met with individuals at his former campaign headquarters in [Breña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bre%C3%B1a) without public record, a potential violation of a recently created, complicated set of transparency regulations.[^cite_note-315] Lobbyist Karelim López would also become entangled with the controversy in Breña after the company Terminex, who she lobbied for, won the Tarata III Bridge Consortium contract worth 255.9 million soles.[^cite_note-IDL_Reporteros-2021-2-311][^cite_note-larepublica-2021-148][^cite_note-316] Audios purportedly obtained at the residence and released by [América Televisión](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Am%C3%A9rica_Televisi%C3%B3n) were criticized and dismissed as a scam.[^cite_note-317] Castillo responded to the impeachment threat stating: "I am not worried about the political noise because the people have chosen me, not the mafias or the corrupt."[^cite_note-IDL_Reporteros-2021-274] The impeachment proceeding did not occur, as 76 voted against proceedings, 46 were in favor, and 4 abstained, with a requirement of 52 favoring proceedings not being obtained.[^cite_note-La_Republica-2021-318] Free Peru ultimately supported Castillo through the process and described the vote as an attempted right-wing coup.[^cite_note-319] Castillo responded to the vote stating: "Brothers and sisters, let's end political crises and work together to achieve a just and supportive Peru."[^cite_note-La_Republica-2021-318]

#### Second removal attempt and protests



Negotiations between President Pedro Castillo and a trade union in April 2022

In February 2022, it was reported that Fujimorists and politicians close to Fujimori organized a meeting at the Casa Andina hotel in Lima with the assistance of the [German liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_liberal) group [Friedrich Naumann Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Naumann_Foundation), with those present including [Maricarmen Alva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maricarmen_Alva), [President of the Congress of the Republic of Peru](57579474.md), discussing plans to remove President Castillo from office.[^cite_note-320] Alva had already shared her readiness to assume the presidency of Peru if Castillo were to be vacated from the position and a leaked [Telegram](41501305.md) group chat of the Board of Directors of Congress that she heads revealed plans coordinated to oust Castillo.[^cite_note-321][^cite_note-322] A second impeachment attempt related to corruption allegations did make it to proceedings in March 2022. On 28 March 2022, Castillo appeared before Congress calling the allegations baseless and for legislators to "vote for democracy" and "against instability", with 55 voting for impeachment, 54 voting against, and 19 abstaining, not reaching the 87 votes necessary for impeaching Castillo.[^cite_note-323]

On 1 April, protesters in [Junín](714854.md) began to riot, looting stores, ATMs and pharmacies, with authorities announcing that 10 were arrested during the riots.[^cite_note-IDL-Reporteros-2020-173] Simultaneously, representatives of the Castillo government travelled through the nation to negotiate with UGTRANM and transportation representatives, though they were turned away.[^cite_note-IDL-Reporteros-2020-173] UGTRANM leaders demanded that the Castillo administration lower fuel prices, though the government had already instituted the Fuel Price Stabilization Fund according to economist Jorge Gonzales Izquierdo in order to prevent diesel fuel price hikes.[^cite_note-IDL-Reporteros-2020-173] The government removed a fuel tax that added about 30% to prices, though fuel stations refused to lower their prices.[^cite_note-324] UGTRANM's Diez Villegas on 2 April announced a national [general strike](41138909.md) to occur on 4 April.[^cite_note-IDL-Reporteros-2020-173]

On 9 April, President Castillo presented a constitutional amendment proposal to reform Article 61 of the Peruvian constitution, sharing plans on what he said would ban "monopolies, oligopolies, hoarding, [speculation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speculation) or price agreements, as well as the abuse of dominant positions in the market" in order to establish a [social market economy.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Market_Economy)[^cite_note-325]

Despite a truce between the government and some groups, unions in Cusco began protests on 18 April demanding the lowering of prices and the rewriting of the Peruvian constitution, resulting with hundreds of tourists being stranded in some areas.[^cite_note-326]

In July 2022, a fifth inquest was launched into Castillo's alleged involvement in corruption.[^cite_note-mercopress-2022-327]

On 5 November, thousands of opponents of the government marched through the capital's center to call for the removal of President Pedro Castillo.[^cite_note-328]

#### Self-coup attempt and removal

Main articles: [Third impeachment and removal of Pedro Castillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_impeachment_and_removal_of_Pedro_Castillo) and [2022 Peruvian self-coup attempt](72438093.md)



Camera footage showing [Aníbal Torres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An%C3%ADbal_Torres) and [Betssy Chávez](72343741.md) moments before Castillo's declaration.

On 8 November 2022, [Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Council_of_Ministers_of_Peru) [Aníbal Torres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An%C3%ADbal_Torres) asked Congress to vote on a matter of confidence to reform Law 31355, which would modify the parliamentary procedure to dissolve congress and call congressional elections. Congress shelved the Executive's request for reform, for which on November 11 Castillo warned: “We have declared before Parliament a prerogative of the Executive, as is the question of trust. Today we know the answer. I must announce that the Executive will also give an answer in the next few hours to the Peruvian people."

On 17 November, Torres once again asked Congress to vote on another question of confidence to repeal another law, 31399, which was approved in January 2022 and which modified the referendum.[^cite_note-329] The Government opposed the rule, alleging that it prevented the participation of citizens directly. On November 24, the Board of Directors of Congress rejected the question of confidence. The president of the Congress, [José Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Williams), explained that the President of the Republic cannot attribute an interpretation contrary to that indicated by the Constitution and the regulations of Parliament, doing so would lead to an unconstitutional dissolution of Congress[^cite_note-330]**.** On the other hand, on 30 November the Constitutional Court declared unfounded the claim of unconstitutionality filed by the Executive Power against Law 31399.[^cite_note-331]

Despite the Government's interpretations of having considered that two questions of trust were allegedly denied, Congressmen and specialists in constitutional law declared that the constitution would be infringed.[^cite_note-El_Comercio_Perú-2022-332] Reforming a law is the exclusive competence of the Legislature and therefore, only Congress can interpret whether or not trust was denied.[^cite_note-El_Comercio_Perú-2022-332]

On 24 November, Torres resigned again. It was accepted and Minister of Culture [Betssy Chávez](72343741.md) was selected by President Castillo to replace him.[^cite_note-333]

On 29 November, the non-grouped congressman [Edward Málaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_M%C3%A1laga) presented the presidential vacancy motion for "moral ineptitude" with 67 signatures, which exceeded the necessary minimum number of 26 signatures.[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335] The debate for the admission of the vacancy motion was held on 1 December.[^cite_note-336] When the date arrived, the motion was admitted to debate with 73 votes in favor, 32 against and 6 abstentions, with which the President of the Republic was summoned to the Plenary on 7 December 2022 to exercise his right to defense.[^cite_note-mocion-337] The vacancy required a qualified majority (two thirds of the legal number of congressmen, which corresponds to 87 votes in favor of 130); however, various voices considered that the votes were not guaranteed to approve the vacancy,[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340] despite this, an unexpected event occurred hours before the session.

On 5 December, just days before Congress was set to vote on impeaching Castillo, a constitutional complaint was filed by the Subcommittee on Constitutional Accusations against Vice President [Dina Boluarte](67870874.md), alleging that she operated a private club while she was the Minister of Development.[^cite_note-Gestión-2022-341] The allegations against Boluarte created the potential for the vice president to face controversy if Castillo were to be impeached.[^cite_note-Gestión-2022-341]

On 6 December, [Chief of the Joint Command](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_of_the_Joint_Command_\(Peru\)) General [Manuel Gómez de la Torre](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel%20G%C3%B3mez%20de%20la%20Torre) held a meeting with the branch heads of the [armed forces of Peru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_forces_of_Peru).[^cite_note-342] During the meeting, General Gómez de la Torre warned the branch heads of a pending conflict, stating "I am responsible. There are no other orders". Commanding general of the [Army of Peru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Army_of_Peru), Wálter Córdova, also submitted his resignation on 6 December, with the submission being approved the next morning.

On 7 December, Congress was expected to file a [motion of censure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motion_of_censure) against Castillo, accusing him of "[permanent moral incapacity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vacancy_due_to_moral_incapacity_\(Peru\))".[^cite_note-CNN-2022-343] Before the legislative body could gather to file its motion, Castillo announced the dissolution of Congress and enacted an immediate [curfew](297681.md).[^cite_note-CNN-2022-343][^cite_note-344] In his speech, Castillo stated:[^cite_note-Chillitupa_Tantas-2022-345][^cite_note-346]

> \[T]he majority of Congress, which defends the interests of the big monopolies and oligopolies, has done everything to try to destroy the institution of the presidency ... The Executive has sent to Congress more than 70 bills of national interest with the aim of benefiting the most vulnerable sectors of the population, such as the Massification of Gas, the creation of the Ministry of Science and Technology and Innovation, Free Admission to Universities, the Second Agrarian Reform, the Tax Reform, the Reform of the Justice System, the elimination of the subsidiary economic activity of the State Congress sought to prosecute the president for treason with unsustainable and absurd arguments from one of constitutionalist jurists, ... Without the slightest evidence, Congress charged the president with crimes many times, often based on claims made by a mercenary, corrupt and cynical press, which defames and slanders in absolute debauchery. However, the congress does not investigate and sanction criminal acts of its own members. Congress has destroyed the rule of law, democracy, separation, and balance of powers by modifying the Constitution with ordinary laws to destroy the Executive and establish a congressional dictatorship with the endorsement, as they themselves state, of the Constitutional Court. ... The following measures are dictated: to temporarily dissolve the Congress of the Republic and establish an exceptional [emergency government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_government). Elections will be called for a new Congress with constituent powers to draft a new Constitution within no more than nine months.

He then called on individuals possessing illegal weapons to deliver them to the [National Police](14896441.md) within 72 hours and ordered all troops of the [Peruvian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peruvian_Army) to remain at their barracks, saying that he did not want clashes between citizens and the military.[^cite_note-Chillitupa_Tantas-2022-345][^cite_note-347]



Document of the [CCFFAA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Command_of_the_Armed_Forces_of_Peru) and [PNP](14896441.md) rejecting the actions of Castillo

The [Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Peru) released a statement following the coup: "No one owes obedience to a usurping government and Mr. Pedro Castillo has made an ineffective coup d'état. The Armed Forces are empowered to restore the constitutional order."[^cite_note-Chillitupa_Tantas-2022-345] The [Armed Forces](23438.md) also issued a statement rejecting Castillo's actions and calling for the maintenance of stability in Peru.[^cite_note-348] Democratic institutions and civil society quickly rejected Castillo's actions,[^cite_note-349] Moments after Castillo's speech, multiple ministers resigned from his government, including Prime Minister [Betssy Chávez](72343741.md).[^cite_note-350]

Rejecting Castillo's actions to dissolve the legislative body, Congress gathered and voted to remove Castillo from office due to "moral incapacity" with 101 votes in favor, 6 against and 10 abstentions.[^cite_note-El_Comercio_(Perú)-2022-351] It was announced that First Vice President [Dina Boluarte](67870874.md), who rejected Castillo's actions, would take her [oath of office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oath_of_office) for the presidency at 3\:00 pm [PET](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_in_Peru).[^cite_note-El_Comercio_(Perú)-2022-351]

President Castillo then fled the [Government Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_Palace,_Peru) and contacted [president of Mexico](24356889.md) [Andrés Manuel López Obrador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Manuel_L%C3%B3pez_Obrador), raising concerns about [political asylum](1621432.md).[^cite_note-RPP-2022-352] According to President López Obrador, it was likely that Castillo's [phone was tapped](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telephone_tapping) by Peruvian intelligence, with the Mexican president reporting that Peruvian authorities entered the grounds of the Mexican embassy to prevent Castillo from entering.[^cite_note-RPP-2022-352] Castillo said that he did not seek to leave Peru and only wanted to drop his family off at the Mexican embassy for their safety. Upon learning that Castillo was seeking to enter the Mexican embassy, the general of the PNP learned that his officers were the drivers for Castillo and ordered them to deliver Castillo to the Prefecture where he would be arrested. Individuals gathered outside the Mexican embassy in Lima to block the area upon rumors that Castillo was attempting to flee to seek asylum in Mexico.[^cite_note-353] The PNP officers driving Castillo said that they had to make an emergency detour, later bringing him to the Prefecture where he was greeted by the head of the PNP, who arrested him, *[in flagrante delicto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In_flagrante_delicto)*, for [rebellion](513246.md).[^cite_note-354][^cite_note-355][^cite_note-356][^cite_note-Reuters22-357][^cite_note-MSN-358]

Castillo's vice president [Dina Boluarte](67870874.md) entered the [Legislative Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_Palace_\(Peru\)) shortly after 3\:00 pm PET and appeared before Congress, where she was later sworn in as president of Peru.[^cite_note-359] Following Castillo's removal, his supporters started [nationwide protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/December_2022_Peruvian_protests) demanding his release and Boluarte's resignation.

While Castillo was detained, he denounced a "[Machiavellian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machiavelli) plan" against him by the National Prosecutor [Patricia Benavides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patricia_Benavides), Congress and his former vice president Boluarte.[^cite_note-360] Some Latin American media organizations linked the events to [United States Ambassador to Peru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Ambassador_to_Peru) [Lisa D. Kenna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisa_D._Kenna), a former member of the [Central Intelligence Agency](5183633.md).[^cite_note-Ultimas_Noticias-2022-361][^cite_note-La_Jornada-2022-362] Ambassador Kenna had met with Castillo's defense minister, Gustavo Bobbio, the day before the impeachment vote, raising concerns about the United States being involved in the events.[^cite_note-Ultimas_Noticias-2022-361][^cite_note-La_Jornada-2022-362] Local media also reported that the President of Congress and former head of the Peruvian armed forces, [José Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Williams), allegedly collaborated with Ambassador Kenna.[^cite_note-Ultimas_Noticias-2022-361]

### Boluarte presidency

#### Protests

Main article: [Peruvian protests (2022–2023)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peruvian_protests_\(2022%E2%80%932023\))



Protesters denouncing the government's use of violence, January 2023

Following Castillo's removal, his supporters started [nationwide protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%932023_Peruvian_protests) demanding his release and Boluarte's resignation.[^cite_note-BBC_News-2022-363] On 14 December, the government declared a 30-day state of emergency.[^cite_note-364] On 16 December, the sixth day of government, the two Ministers of Education and Culture Patricia Correa and Jair Pérez announced their resignation, in opposition to the outbreak of the state of emergency in the country and the numerous deaths during the demonstrations in favor of Pedro Castillo,[^cite_note-365] whose death toll worsened the same day, with 18 dead, confirmed by the Minister of Health Rosa Gutiérrez.[^cite_note-366] Protests erupted [into violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%932023_Apur%C3%ADmac_insurgency) on 11 December near the southern city of [Andahuaylas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andahuaylas) where demonstrators closed the airport, with police in a helicopter reportedly firing upon protesters, killing two individuals.[^cite_note-367] President Boluarte attempted to appease protests by proposing elections two years early, for April 2024, though Castillo supporters rejected the call while Castillo described such actions as a "dirty game".[^cite_note-BBC_News-2022-363] Congress would reject Boluarte's proposal for early elections.[^cite_note-dw-2022-368] However, Congress reconsidered the proposal on 21 December 2022 and allowed the discussion of early elections to begin in 2023.[^cite_note-369] Police brutality against protesters led to the [Juliaca massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juliaca_massacre) on 9 January 2023. Attorney General of Peru [Patricia Benavides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patricia_Benavides) announced investigations the following day to determine if Boluarte, Prime Minister [Alberto Otárola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Ot%C3%A1rola), Minister of the Interior Víctor Rojas, and Minister of Defense Jorge Chávez committed [genocide](12441.md) and [aggravated homicide](20171.md).[^cite_note-370] Groups in Congress opposed to Boluarte opened an impeachment motion against her on 25 January, citing moral incapacity.[^cite_note-371]

##### Violence

See also: [2022–2023 Apurímac protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%932023_Apur%C3%ADmac_protests)

Violence during the protests were often concentrated in rural areas, which were considered a stronghold for protests. Many protesters declared themselves in an [insurgency](589841.md),[^cite_note-diarioexpresion-2022-372] while the [rondas campesinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rondas_campesinas) led a peaceful campaign of strikes against the government.[^cite_note-373] Both the [Peruvian Armed Forces](23438.md) and the [Peruvian Police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_enforcement_in_Peru) widely suppressed the protests, often with impunity.



Protests against [Fujimorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fujimorism) and the Boluarte administration in July 2023

The region of [Puno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Puno) was heavily affected during the violence, especially during the [Juliaca massacre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juliaca_massacre), where eighteen people were killed by the [National Police of Peru](14896441.md).[^cite_note-374][^cite_note-375] Puno also suffered directly from [blockades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blockades), [looting](209811.md), and other types of unrest. [Apurímac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Apur%C3%ADmac) also suffered heavily during the unrest. Protests in the city of [Andahuaylas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andahuaylas) exploded into violence on 11 December 2022 followed by ensuing clashes by the [Peruvian Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peruvian_Army) and local protesters in the city.[^cite_note-diarioexpresion-2022-372] The [Madre de Dios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Madre_de_Dios) region in the southwest was also blockaded by protesters, with the blockade eventually being lifted by the Peruvian Armed Forces. Other clashes and deadly incidents took place in the regions of [Ica](713288.md), [Arequipa](713212.md), [Ayacucho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Ayacucho), and [Junín](714854.md).[^cite_note-ojo-publico-2022-376][^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378][^cite_note-379]

#### Fujimorists' consolidation of power

[Fujimorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fujimorism) would ultimately consolidate governmental power into Congress with the assistance of the [Constitutional Court of Peru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Peru).[^cite_note-Romero-35][^cite_note-Ni_siquiera_topo:_apenas_tapón-33][^cite_note-380] The Constitutional Court would remove judicial oversight of Congress, essentially giving the legislative body absolute control of Peru's government.[^cite_note-Romero-35][^cite_note-larepublica.pe-36] The politicization of the armed forces also raised concerns about a developing [civilian-military government](216815.md) in Peru.[^cite_note-larepublica-2023-50][^cite_note-larepublica-2023a-51] Congress, with one-third of its members belonging to a [far-right](221220.md) bloc,[^cite_note-FAR-32] would reject all attempts of reform, including the advancing of general elections, constitutional proposals and motions to impeach Boluarte.[^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382] while the [Supreme Court of Peru](8801058.md) would further rule on 18 May 2023 that protesting in Peru was illegal and that it was not protected by the constitution.[^cite_note-383][^cite_note-384] Calls for future protests in July 2023 were made amidst the controversial verdict of the Supreme Court.[^cite_note-385]

By May 2023, Fujimorists gained control of the [Office of the Public Defender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peruvian_ombudsman) and other institutions.[^cite_note-Ni_siquiera_topo:_apenas_tapón-33][^cite_note-Un_defensor_del_pueblo-386] In September 2023, Fujimorists in Congress would attempt to restructure the [National Board of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Board_of_Justice) (JNJ).[^cite_note-\:LRPC1-387][^cite_note-\:LRPC2-388] The move was condemned as a "parliamentary coup" by newspaper *[La República](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Rep%C3%BAblica)* and by NGOs like the Center for Justice and International Law (CEJIL), Due Process of Law Foundation, Fundación Construir, Fundación para la Justicia y el Estado Democrático de Derecho (FJEDD), [Human Rights Watch](46530.md), the [International Commission of Jurists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Commission_of_Jurists), Observatorio Derechos y Justicia (ODJ), [Robert F. Kennedy Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_F._Kennedy_Human_Rights) and the [Washington Office on Latin America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Office_on_Latin_America), with the latter group demanding the application of the [Inter-American Democratic Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inter-American_Democratic_Charter).[^cite_note-\:LRPC1-387][^cite_note-\:LRPC2-388]

On 30 July 2024, Boluarte's government was challenged by Peru's top prosecutor office which filed a complaint against Boluarte's government for the murder of 44 protestors during the 2022–2023 protests.[^cite_note-389] On 12 August 2024, Peru passed a bill which prevented the prosecution of crimes against humanity committed by government between 1980 and 2000, in what was seen as a bill which would benefit former dictator Alberto Fujimori who was in power between 1990 and 2000 and planned to stand in the 2026 elections, though he would later die a month later after the bill was passed.[^cite_note-390][^cite_note-391]

### Jeri and Balcázar presidencies



José Jerí meeting clandestinely with Chinese businessman Zhihua Yang in January 2026.

Amidst the [2025 Peruvian protests](81215985.md) and after the unanimous [impeachment and removal](81305691.md) of [Dina Boluarte](67870874.md) on 10 October 2025, owing to persistent political scandals throughout her presidency and a mismanaged crime wave earlier in the month, [José Jerí](80709159.md) immediately ascended as [President of Peru](239109.md). With Boluarte having no vice presidents, Jerí, as president of the legislature, stood next in the line of succession established in the Peruvian Constitution.[^cite_note-reuters-20251010-392][^cite_note-393][^cite_note-394] He became the eighth individual to serve as President since the resignation of [Pedro Pablo Kuczynski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Pablo_Kuczynski) in 2018, a span of just over seven years.[^cite_note-395]

Jeri presidency was highly controversial and lasted only four months. On 14 February 2026, it was announced that Jerí was under investigation for [illicit enrichment](69979637.md) for a second time. He was accused of [influence peddling](630570.md) after the revelation of secretive meetings with Chinese businessmen outside of any official framework.[^cite_note-396] Three days later, on 17 February, Jerí was removed from the presidency by the Peruvian Congress in a 75-24 vote, with 3 abstentions, following a motion of censure approved weeks before the general elections.[^cite_note-CNN-397] He was removed as President of Congress after he failed to disclose meetings with Chinese businessmen who were under government scrutiny. He was only president for four months.[^cite_note-NYT2026-398]

[José María Balcázar](82451696.md) was sworn in as his successor until the [general election](69679018.md) which [Keiko Fujimori](4643445.md) won.[^cite_note-399] A [post-election crisis](82959540.md) followed as a result of logistical problems on the part of the [National Office of Electoral Processes](5446067.md) (ONPE) in the Lima Metropolitan Region. The far-right party [Popular Renewal](66197743.md) (Renovación Popular) attempted to overturn the results, claiming a supposed electoral fraud, but this had no results.



Results of the 2026 elections. The regions that supported Castillo in 2021 were the same ones that supported Sánchez.

As in the 2021 elections, almost no candidate managed to reach even 10% of the vote. The leading candidates, Keiko Fujimori and [Roberto Sánchez](72806167.md), received only 14.26% and 9.99%, respectively (17% and 12% of the valid votes). In fact, the amount of blank and invalid votes (17%) was higher that any of the candidates,[^cite_note-400] just as in the 2021 election.[^cite_note-401] The fragmentation was exacerbated by the excessive number of political parties. There were a total of 36 parties, twice as in 2021. This was a direct consequence of the Boluarte administration, which eliminated the "PASO" elections (Open, Simultaneous, and Mandatory Primary elections), a filter created precisely to prevent such scenario.[^cite_note-402][^cite_note-403] The decision was criticized by several experts, as it limited citizen participation and would result in extreme fragmentation,[^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405][^cite_note-406][^cite_note-número_alto_de_partidos-407] making it so that the next president's political representation would be "5 or 10% at most".[^cite_note-408]

The second round between Keiko Fujimori and Roberto Sánchez was once again marked by intense polarization. Fujimori ultimately won the presidency by just 0.26% of the vote—roughly 40,000 votes—while Sánchez refused to recognize the result, extending the post-election dispute.[^cite_note-409]

### Keiko Fujimori presidency

On 28 July 2026, Keiko Fujimori was sworn in as Peru’s president.[^cite_note-410]

## Effects

### Economic

During the 2023 unrest, the main economic sectors of the Peruvian economy largely ceased during the protests.[^cite_note-Garcia-2023-411] Three weeks into protests, the National Chamber of Tourism (Canatur) reported that due to protests, Peru had lost an estimated 1.7 billion [soles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peruvian_sol)(\$450 million USD) of tourism income since the start of the demonstrations.[^cite_note-412] A month into protests, some stores in Lima were reporting a 60% decrease in sales compared to the previous year.[^cite_note-La_Republica-2023i-413] According to the Boluarte government, Peru experienced \$1.3 billion of infrastructure damage from protests by late January.[^cite_note-Garcia-2023-411] On 31 January 2023, [Moody's Investors Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moody's_Investors_Service) changed Peru's credit rating outlook to "Negative", citing "a deterioration in institutional cohesion, governability, policy effectiveness and economic strength through successive governments".[^cite_note-414] In August 2023, it was announced that the country had entered a [recession](25382.md).

### Foreign Policy



Nations recognizing presidential governments\
&#x20; [Pedro Castillo](49637220.md)\
&#x20; [Dina Boluarte](67870874.md)

During the 2020 protests, both [El Salvador](9356.md) and [Venezuela](32374.md) refused to recognize the legitimacy of Manuel Merino's government,[^cite_note-415] with Salvadoran President [Nayib Bukele](46452145.md) calling it a "government of coup plotters".[^cite_note-416]

After Castillo's removal from power, a new shakeup occurred in regards to Peru's foreign relations after [Mexico](3966054.md) said that it would continue to recognize Castillo as Peru's president.[^cite_note-hilare-2022-417] Other countries, including [Colombia](5222.md), [Venezuela](32374.md), [Honduras](13394.md), [Argentina](18951905.md), and [Cuba](5042481.md), recognized Castillo as the legitimate president of Peru following the events of the [constitutional crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Peruvian_political_crisis).[^cite_note-hilare-2022-417][^cite_note-418][^cite_note-419] Following unrest, relations with the United States would also sour following a letter from 15 members of the [United States Congress](31756.md) that criticized the Peruvian government and the Congress of the Republic due to their undemocratic nominations of the Constitutional Court and the Ombudsman Office.[^cite_note-Vega-41] Throughout 2023, [United States](3434750.md) [President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_united_States) [Joe Biden](145422.md) would also be criticized due to his support for the Boluarte government.[^cite_note-420][^cite_note-421][^cite_note-422][^cite_note-423] Relations with Spain and Brazil soured as well following a rejection of arms sales due to violence.[^cite_note-424]

### Democratic backsliding and corruption

|  | It has been suggested that this section be [**split**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Splitting) out into another article titled *[Democratic backsliding in Peru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding_in_Peru)*. ([**Discuss**](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Peruvian_political_crisis_\(2016%E2%80%93present\))) *(July 2026)* |
| --- | --- |

See also: [Democratic backsliding in the Americas by country § Peru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding_in_the_Americas_by_country#Peru), and [Corruption in Peru](41862074.md)

Since the turmoil began, Peru has undergone significant [democratic backsliding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding) during the presidencies of [Manuel Merino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Merino),[^cite_note-The_Wire-16][^cite_note-Delfino-44] [Pedro Castillo](49637220.md),[^cite_note-Peru's_Democracy_Is_Dying-45][^cite_note-washingtonpost.com-46] and [Dina Boluarte](67870874.md),[^cite_note-AUTHOR-18] with those governments often being described as [authoritarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Authoritarian) and [corrupt](3608404.md). Even during the presidencies of [Pedro Pablo Kuczynski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Pablo_Kuczynski), [Martín Vizcarra](51682201.md), and [Francisco Sagasti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Sagasti), Peru was seen as a backsliding democracy due to the obstructionist efforts of the [Congress of the Republic](1571007.md).[^cite_note-425]



A billboard during the 2021 general elections supporting Fujimorism and Fujimori's fearmongering campaign

During the presidency of Kuczynski, Congress's obstructionist efforts and corruption scandals plagued his public image and was evident as seen following the [Pardon of Alberto Fujimori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pardon_of_Alberto_Fujimori).[^cite_note-426] Kuczynski was also under investigation due to his links to the [Odebrecht scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Car_Wash) and other [offshore monetary affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Offshore_financial_centre).[^cite_note-fowks-2018-427][^cite_note-428] Corruption within the state continued following Fujimori's pardon, and following Kuczynski's second impeachment attempt, he would resign following a vote buying scandal.[^cite_note-diariocorreo-102] As a result, his successor Martín Vizcarra would lead an anti-corruption campaign, which was heavily obstructed by Congress.[^cite_note-taj-2018-105][^cite_note-429] However, it would be revealed in 2020 that Vizcarra himself had been implicated in several corruption scandals during his early political career, which would result in his removal. The ascension of Merino to the presidency was controversial, as it was believed Vizcarra's impeachment was done for political favors. Merino would go on and form a right-wing government and would repress [protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Peruvian_protests) harshly, after which he would resign under pressure. Merino was criticized even more after he left office due to the apparent use of [police brutality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Police_brutality) during the unrest.[^cite_note-430][^cite_note-431]
